# Banking / Finance — INTRODUCTION

[All topics](README.md) · [Tables and sample rows](DOMAIN_GUIDE.md)

Write the SQL for each question. Use your practice database. For exercises that change existing tables or data, start with the original sample data; ask your instructor to reset the practice tables between exercises.

## Relational database fundamentals

### INTRODUCTION-01-02

Banking / Finance: For Transactions and Accounts, explain how relational database fundamentals addresses duplicate master details in every transaction. Name two relevant database concepts.

### INTRODUCTION-01-07

Banking / Finance: Design a small Transactions and Accounts example illustrating this failure: duplicate master details in every transaction. Propose a database-level response and explain one limitation.

### INTRODUCTION-01-12

Banking / Finance: Review this proposal for Transactions and Accounts: "Let the application handle everything; no database design or validation is needed." Evaluate it specifically for relational database fundamentals.

### INTRODUCTION-01-17

Banking / Finance: Write an acceptance-test plan for Transactions and Accounts that would detect duplicate master details in every transaction. Include setup, action and an observable pass condition.

### INTRODUCTION-01-22

Banking / Finance: Write a short design-review note for Transactions and Accounts: explain duplicate master details in every transaction, recommend a control, define a success measure and identify one follow-up question for the owner.

## Levelled interview practice

Each subtopic below has15 questions per level. Each ID identifies the complete task,analysis rule or core case,and edge conditions. The earlier questions retain their original IDs as foundation practice.

### Relational database fundamentals: progression

**Beginner:15 questions**

#### BANKING-INTRODUCTION-01-B01

**Valid baseline** · Beginner · Suggested coding time:15 minutes

**Core task:** Use `[banking].[Transactions]` and `[banking].[Accounts]` to demonstrate relational database fundamentals through a concrete failure and a corrected native/local implementation. Use at least two rows with distinct stable keys. Return the failure outcome,the corrected outcome and an assertion proving the business rule. Definition lists alone do not satisfy this task.

**Interview case:** Implement the core in a fresh practice copy. Return the requested result and a before/after object or value snapshot. Explain which business rule it establishes.

**Required evidence:** Return a request/disposition table(InputOrdinal,Decision,Reason),a keyed before/after state or value table,and an assertion table(CheckName,Expected,Actual,Passed). For a design choice left open,declare it before implementation and test it. Unsupported operations/types must be rejected explicitly rather than silently simulated as native SQL features.

**Environment:** Use a separate practice copy;changes persist. Ask the instructor to reset the original schema/data between independent cases. No transaction/rollback commands are required prerequisites. Principal/database provisioning needs an instructor-provided disposable environment;until then,use a clearly labelled finite local model.

**Grading boundary:** This is a practical design/native-demonstration exercise,not a single-output automatic test. An explanation without the concrete case,implementation and observed evidence is incomplete.

#### BANKING-INTRODUCTION-01-B02

**Intermediate:15 questions**

#### BANKING-INTRODUCTION-01-I01

**Batch with mixed validity** · Intermediate · Suggested coding time:30 minutes

**Core task:** Use `[banking].[Transactions]` and `[banking].[Accounts]` to demonstrate relational database fundamentals through a concrete failure and a corrected native/local implementation. Use at least two rows with distinct stable keys. Return the failure outcome,the corrected outcome and an assertion proving the business rule. Definition lists alone do not satisfy this task.

**Valid baseline** · Beginner · Suggested coding time:15 minutes

**Core task:** Use `[banking].[Transactions]` and `[banking].[Accounts]` to demonstrate enterprise applications through a concrete failure and a corrected native/local implementation. Use at least two rows with distinct stable keys. Return the failure outcome,the corrected outcome and an assertion proving the business rule. Definition lists alone do not satisfy this task.

**Interview case:** Implement the core in a fresh practice copy. Return the requested result and a before/after object or value snapshot. Explain which business rule it establishes.

**Required evidence:** Return a request/disposition table(InputOrdinal,Decision,Reason),a keyed before/after state or value table,and an assertion table(CheckName,Expected,Actual,Passed). For a design choice left open,declare it before implementation and test it. Unsupported operations/types must be rejected explicitly rather than silently simulated as native SQL features.

**Environment:** Use a separate practice copy;changes persist. Ask the instructor to reset the original schema/data between independent cases. No transaction/rollback commands are required prerequisites. Principal/database provisioning needs an instructor-provided disposable environment;until then,use a clearly labelled finite local model.

**Grading boundary:** This is a practical design/native-demonstration exercise,not a single-output automatic test. An explanation without the concrete case,implementation and observed evidence is incomplete.
